I’ll be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I in the middle of?” It’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or losing your train of thought easily in other situations, like when you’re at home or talking with friends, or is this something that only happens at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, work is definitely where I notice it the most, because the floor is just… constant. Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.

I’ll literally be standing there like, what was I doing?\n\nI’m sure it happens other places too, but the main reason I’m here is that at work it’s constant and it’s really messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home or in a quieter environment — maybe watching TV, reading, or talking with someone — and you suddenly lost your train of thought the same way you described at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m trying to think of a specific home example, but honestly my mind keeps going back to the floor because that’s where it’s really obvious. Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.

And I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.

It’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?

I’ll literally be standing there like, what was I doing?\n\nI’m sure it happens other places too, but the main reason I’m here is that at work it’s constant and it’s really messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home or in a quieter environment — maybe watching TV, reading, or talking with someone — and you suddenly lost your train of thought the same way you described at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m trying to think of a specific home example, but honestly my mind keeps going back to the floor because that’s where it’s really obvious. Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I’ll be trying to sort out a patient flow issue, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like—whatever I was just doing is gone.\n\nAnd I know hospitals are noisy, that’s normal, but this feels… excessive. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I in the middle of?” It’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or losing your train of thought easily in other situations, like when you’re at home or talking with friends, or is this something that only happens at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, work is definitely where I notice it the most, because the floor is just… constant. Like I’ll be right in the middle of figuring out a patient flow thing, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly it’s just gone. I’ll literally be standing there like, what was I doing?\n\nI’m sure it happens other places too, but the main reason I’m here is that at work it’s constant and it’s really messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home or in a quieter environment — maybe watching TV, reading, or talking with someone — and you suddenly lost your train of thought the same way you described at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m trying to think of a specific home example, but honestly my mind keeps going back to the floor because that’s where it’s really obvious. Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as “on the go” in that way? Like, I’m tired a lot of the time when I’m trying to relax, so I’ll sit down and then—well, sometimes I’m mentally jumping around, but physically restless… I don’t know.\n\nI might tap my foot or mess with my hands if I’m waiting somewhere, but I’m not sure if that’s more than normal. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting without thinking, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can't wait your turn?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I in the middle of?” It’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or losing your train of thought easily in other situations, like when you’re at home or talking with friends, or is this something that only happens at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, work is definitely where I notice it the most, because the floor is just… constant. Like I’ll be right in the middle of figuring out a patient flow thing, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly it’s just gone. I’ll literally be standing there like, what was I doing?\n\nI’m sure it happens other places too, but the main reason I’m here is that at work it’s constant and it’s really messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home or in a quieter environment — maybe watching TV, reading, or talking with someone — and you suddenly lost your train of thought the same way you described at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m trying to think of a specific home example, but honestly my mind keeps going back to the floor because that’s where it’s really obvious. Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as “on the go” in that way? Like, I’m tired a lot of the time when I’m trying to relax, so I’ll sit down and then—well, sometimes I’m mentally jumping around, but physically restless… I don’t know.\n\nI might tap my foot or mess with my hands if I’m waiting somewhere, but I’m not sure if that’s more than normal. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting without thinking, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can't wait your turn?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m the kind of person who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off, at least not that I notice.\n\nSometimes in conversation I’ll jump in because I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but then I’ll realize I maybe interrupted, and I feel bad. But I don’t know if that’s, like, an impulse thing or just normal conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn… I mean, I don’t love waiting in lines, but who does? Actually, the grocery store line near my apartment is always weirdly slow because they only staff one lane, and then I start looking at all the random little snacks by the register and remember I forgot toothpaste—sorry. Um, no, I guess I haven’t really thought of myself as acting without thinking in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get tired, but I also work weird shifts sometimes, so it’s hard to tell what’s “unusual” versus just… life, you know?\n\nLike, after a long day I’ll feel wiped out, but I don’t know that it happens when I haven’t done anything taxing. And then sometimes I’ll be sitting on the couch and feel drained, but I’ll realize I haven’t eaten properly or I stayed up scrolling—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t specifically noticed low energy out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem entirely fair?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I feel guilty about little things sometimes, like if I forgot to call someone back or I left laundry in the washer too long and then it smells weird—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been blaming myself more than usual, though. At work I’ll replay conversations in my head sometimes, but I think a lot of nurses do that because you’re always trying not to miss anything. So… I guess I haven’t noticed guilt being a big thing or anything that feels unfair.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I feel guilty about little things sometimes, like if I forgot to call someone back or I left laundry in the washer too long and then it smells weird—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been blaming myself more than usual, though. At work I’ll replay conversations in my head sometimes, but I think a lot of nurses do that because you’re always trying not to miss anything. So… I guess I haven’t noticed guilt being a big thing or anything that feels unfair.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed yourself getting more irritable or frustrated than usual, even over small things, either at work or at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I mean, I get annoyed, sure, especially at work when things are busy or someone leaves supplies in the wrong place and then you’re hunting for flushes or tape or whatever. But I don’t think I’ve noticed being more irritable than usual.

At home, little things can bug me, like dishes in the sink, but then I’ll remember I also left half of them there, so it’s not exactly fair for me to get mad. And sometimes I’m just hungry or I haven’t had coffee, which makes everything feel more irritating than it probably is.

So… I guess no, not in a way that stands out to me.

Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I in the middle of?” It’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or losing your train of thought easily in other situations, like when you’re at home or talking with friends, or is this something that only happens at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, work is definitely where I notice it the most, because the floor is just… constant. Like I’ll be right in the middle of figuring out a patient flow thing, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as “on the go” in that way? Like, I’m tired a lot of the time when I’m trying to relax, so I’ll sit down and then—well, sometimes I’m mentally jumping around, but physically restless… I don’t know.\n\nI might tap my foot or mess with my hands if I’m waiting somewhere, but I’m not sure if that’s more than normal. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting without thinking, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can't wait your turn?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m the kind of person who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off, at least not that I notice.\n\nSometimes in conversation I’ll jump in because I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but then I’ll realize I maybe interrupted, and I feel bad. Actually, the grocery store line near my apartment is always weirdly slow because they only staff one lane, and then I start looking at all the random little snacks by the register and remember I forgot toothpaste—sorry. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Yeah… I think so. I mean, my mom would probably have a whole file on this if you asked her.

In elementary school my teachers always wrote stuff like I was bright but careless, or that I needed to slow down, and I was constantly losing worksheets. Like they’d be in my backpack somewhere, or shoved in a folder with old permission slips and snack wrappers—my backpack was always this disaster zone.

And homework was a big thing. I’d do it sometimes and then just… not turn it in, which sounds ridiculous, but I really would forget. My mom had to sit with me pretty much every night to keep me on track, otherwise I’d drift off or get stuck sharpening pencils or reorganizing erasers or something. So yes, it was definitely there when I was a kid.

I’ll be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I in the middle of?” It’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or losing your train of thought easily in other situations, like when you’re at home or talking with friends, or is this something that only happens at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, work is definitely where I notice it the most, because the floor is just… constant. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as “on the go” in that way? Like, I’m tired a lot of the time when I’m trying to relax, so I’ll sit down and then—well, sometimes I’m mentally jumping around, but physically restless… I don’t know.\n\nI might tap my foot or mess with my hands if I’m waiting somewhere, but I’m not sure if that’s more than normal. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting without thinking, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can't wait your turn?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m the kind of person who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off, at least not that I notice.\n\nSometimes in conversation I’ll jump in because I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but then I’ll realize I maybe interrupted, and I feel bad. But I don’t know if that’s, like, an impulse thing or just normal conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn… I mean, I don’t love waiting in lines, but who does? Actually, the grocery store line near my apartment is always weirdly slow because they only staff one lane, and then I start looking at all the random little snacks by the register and remember I forgot toothpaste—sorry. Um, no, I guess I haven’t really thought of myself as acting without thinking in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. At work I’ll replay conversations in my head sometimes, but I think a lot of nurses do that because you’re always trying not to miss anything. I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still, exactly. Like, I can sit through a meal or a meeting if I have to, but I probably do mess with things—my pen, my badge reel, whatever’s in front of me.

Like I’ll be working through a patient flow issue, trying to figure out who needs what next, and then an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or someone near me asks another nurse something, and it’s like someone wiped the board clean. I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. Yeah… unfortunately, yes. Work is the big one. I mean, I try so hard to compensate—I have lists, reminders, I’ll rewrite things in different colors, which sounds like I’m in middle school with gel pens, but it helps for maybe five minutes.

But it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. And when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through, like making sure I didn’t miss something that I was supposed to circle back to. Which is… honestly really embarrassing.

Because I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping everything moving, and instead I feel like people are watching me because they’re worried I’ll drop something. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, even though I’m really trying.

I just completely lose what I was doing.\n\nAt home, I guess it probably happens, but it doesn’t feel as urgent, you know? At work it’s constant, and it really throws me off because I can’t just pause everything and retrace my steps.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or reading, do you find it hard to focus or stay engaged, or do you usually feel able to follow along without losing track?\n\nPatient: I mean… with TV or reading, it’s harder for me to pin down because if I lose the thread, I can just rewind or reread a paragraph, and it doesn’t feel as dramatic.\n\nBut the main thing—the reason I’m here—is on the floor. I’ll be in the middle of a patient flow issue, like figuring out what needs to happen next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse something nearby, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was working on. Like it just disappears. That’s the part that’s happening constantly and really messing me up.\n\nDoctor: When you’re at home doing something quiet and low-pressure, like watching TV, reading, or talking with a friend, do you ever find yourself losing focus or getting distracted easily, even if it’s not as intense as at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, sometimes, probably… like if I’m watching something and my phone buzzes or there’s noise outside, I’ll kind of drift and then realize I missed whatever just happened. But it’s hard for me to separate that from normal stuff, because at home I can just rewind it or ask, “Wait, what did you say?”\n\nThe thing that keeps sticking in my head is work, because there I can’t just rewind. I’ll be on the floor trying to sort out some patient flow thing—like who needs to go where or what needs to happen next—and then an IV pump beeps, or a call light goes off, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, and it’s just… gone. I completely lose the thread of what I was doing. That’s the part that feels constant and really stressful.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any instances in the past, such as during school or other jobs, where you had trouble keeping your focus, especially when tasks required sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… school, definitely. I mean, it’s funny because I always think of this as a work problem now, but if I go back, even in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like, “She’s bright, but careless,” which—ugh, I hated that word. Or they’d say I wasn’t turning things in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it. I’d lose the worksheet, or it would be shoved in my desk or my backpack, or I’d forget to hand in the homework even if my mom had watched me do it the night before. She used to have to sit with me every night to keep me on track, because otherwise I’d just… drift off or get stuck halfway through.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off, but yeah, it goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know that I’d say hopeless. I mean, work has been stressing me out a lot, so sometimes I come home pretty wiped and I don’t feel like doing much, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing.\n\nLike, I still enjoy things when I actually get myself there—seeing a friend, watching something, going for a walk or whatever. Although then I’ll get distracted thinking about laundry or some random thing I forgot to buy, and then I’m in the kitchen opening cabinets for no reason… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down all the time or losing interest in everything. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed than depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you also notice these kinds of attention problems in your day-to-day life at home — like losing things, forgetting tasks, or getting easily sidetracked while doing routine activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. At home it shows up a lot, honestly. I’ll forget things that should be basic, like calling my sister back or paying a bill, even though I fully meant to do it.\n\nAnd I do the thing where I set a reminder, but then the reminder goes off while I’m in the middle of something else, and I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll do it in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll miss an appointment because I had it in my head and then somehow didn’t connect it to the actual day.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t care. I really do mean to follow through. I just get sidetracked so easily, and then later I’m like, wait, how did I forget that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you're constantly 'on the go' even when you're trying to relax or stay still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as “on the go” in that way? Like, I’m tired a lot of the time when I’m trying to relax, so I’ll sit down and then—well, sometimes I’m mentally jumping around, but physically restless… I don’t know.\n\nI might tap my foot or mess with my hands if I’m waiting somewhere, but I’m not sure if that’s more than normal. My coworkers joke that I always have a pen in my hand, but nurses kind of do that anyway, so… yeah, I don’t know if I’d call it constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself acting without thinking, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can't wait your turn?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m the kind of person who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off, at least not that I notice.\n\nSometimes in conversation I’ll jump in because I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but then I’ll realize I maybe interrupted, and I feel bad. But I don’t know if that’s, like, an impulse thing or just normal conversation.\n\nWaiting my turn… I mean, I don’t love waiting in lines, but who does? Actually, the grocery store line near my apartment is always weirdly slow because they only staff one lane, and then I start looking at all the random little snacks by the register and remember I forgot toothpaste—sorry. Um, no, I guess I haven’t really thought of myself as acting without thinking in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get tired, but I also work weird shifts sometimes, so it’s hard to tell what’s “unusual” versus just… life, you know?\n\nLike, after a long day I’ll feel wiped out, but I don’t know that it happens when I haven’t done anything taxing. And then sometimes I’ll be sitting on the couch and feel drained, but I’ll realize I haven’t eaten properly or I stayed up scrolling—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t specifically noticed low energy out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem entirely fair?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I feel guilty about little things sometimes, like if I forgot to call someone back or I left laundry in the washer too long and then it smells weird—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I’ve been blaming myself more than usual, though. At work I’ll replay conversations in my head sometimes, but I think a lot of nurses do that because you’re always trying not to miss anything. So… I guess I haven’t noticed guilt being a big thing or anything that feels unfair.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more irritable or frustrated than usual, even over small things, either at work or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get annoyed, sure, especially at work when things are busy or someone leaves supplies in the wrong place and then you’re hunting for flushes or tape or whatever. But I don’t think I’ve noticed being more irritable than usual.\n\nAt home, little things can bug me, like dishes in the sink, but then I’ll remember I also left half of them there, so it’s not exactly fair for me to get mad. And sometimes I’m just hungry or I haven’t had coffee, which makes everything feel more irritating than it probably is.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your mood felt unusually elevated, irritable, or energized for no clear reason, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I have days where I get kind of wired, but usually there’s a reason, like too much coffee or a busy shift where you just don’t sit down for twelve hours and then somehow your body doesn’t know how to stop. And then I’ll come home and start cleaning random things, like the top of the fridge, which I never notice normally—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nUnusually elevated… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods like that for no clear reason. Irritable, maybe if I’m stressed or tired, but not like out of nowhere that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to get started on tasks, keep things organized, or follow through to finish something once you’ve begun?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Especially the organizing and follow-through part. Like when I’m charge nurse, I’ll think I’m being organized because I have scraps of paper and sticky notes and half-started lists all over the place, but then it’s like I can’t figure out what actually needs to happen first.\n\nSo I end up just reacting to whatever is loudest or most urgent in the moment, and then by the end of the shift I’m rushing around trying to finish everything I meant to do earlier. And then I’ll find some note I wrote to myself like three hours ago and be like, oh, right, that was important.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if these attention and organizational difficulties were present when you were a child, before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my mom would probably have a whole file on this if you asked her.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers always wrote stuff like I was bright but careless, or that I needed to slow down, and I was constantly losing worksheets. Like they’d be in my backpack somewhere, or shoved in a folder with old permission slips and snack wrappers—my backpack was always this disaster zone.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing. I’d do it sometimes and then just… not turn it in, which sounds ridiculous, but I really would forget. My mom had to sit with me pretty much every night to keep me on track, otherwise I’d drift off or get stuck sharpening pencils or reorganizing erasers or something. So yes, it was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling physically restless or fidgety to the point that it was difficult to sit still for extended periods, such as during meetings, meals, or travel?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still, exactly. Like, I can sit through a meal or a meeting if I have to, but I probably do mess with things—my pen, my badge reel, whatever’s in front of me.\n\nTravel is weird because I get annoyed being stuck, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… everyone hates airports, right? I once spent like forty minutes walking around a terminal because my gate changed and then I got distracted by one of those little stores that sells neck pillows and weirdly expensive trail mix—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, maybe, but I’m not sure it’s to the point where I couldn’t sit still for long periods.\n\nDoctor: Have your attention difficulties ever caused problems in your personal relationships or led to any negative consequences at work, like errors or reprimands, despite your best efforts to manage them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. Work is the big one. I mean, I try so hard to compensate—I have lists, reminders, I’ll rewrite things in different colors, which sounds like I’m in middle school with gel pens, but it helps for maybe five minutes.\n\nBut it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. And when I’m charge nurse, other nurses have to double-check parts of my follow-through, like making sure I didn’t miss something that I was supposed to circle back to. Which is… honestly really embarrassing.\n\nBecause I’m supposed to be the person leading and keeping everything moving, and instead I feel like people are watching me because they’re worried I’ll drop something. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, even though I’m really trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
